ParlaMint-DK_20170504101325	Thank you for that, President. The legislative proposal here is another step towards road equality on our islands, that is, the fundamental principle that it should not cost more to use the blue road than to use the asphalt road when visiting our islands. The measure has been under way for many years now, and in relation to this one can always become a little impatient, but I would like to thank the many parties who have been involved in this work and the result that we have finally arrived at here is a result which, if you count both the financial laws and the other agreements that have been made, together gives some 140 million. kr., to really get started on this. It is an amount very similar to the amount proposed by the former SR government in its proposal for a growth package. At that time, we were told that it was very unambitious. Now it has been two years and the current government has finally come up to the same amount. I will spend no more time on that than to say that we are here now. I think it is important to say that the fact that we are here for me is not the end goal. No, it is a stage in which we are now, for the money we have been able to find, but that does not end the work for us in the Social Democratic Party. After all, we can see that with the first money pools that have come out, we have already been able to gain very, very good experiences around the islands. And it simply makes a difference when it becomes affordable for an ordinary Danish family to go on holiday and visit our beautiful islands. Denmark is an island kingdom and it has been the case for far too long that it has simply been too expensive to visit the islands. This has meant that the settlement has become more difficult, it has meant that tourism has stumbled, and this has meant that for a typical Danish family it has often been cheaper to go on a charter trip for an extended weekend in a European city than it has been to see what kind of country we live in and get to know our islands. With this proposal, things are moving in the other direction, in the better direction, in other words, that it is better for our islands to make tourist movements and that it is better for our families to visit our islands. That is why we in the Socialist Group are voting in favour. We are also voting in favour of, and are pleased with, the part relating to the VAT bill and that a solution will be found to it, and I also think it is sensible that the proposal here extends the period of the year in which this principle can be introduced. It is also important to say that with this bill it is not like this at least for me not to see that we have then introduced traffic equality in Denmark. We don't. It is a linked traffic equality, and it is only for a few months of the year. So it is still the case that there is still a long way to go for full transport equality. But I do not want to sprinkle wormwood in the cup today, but I would like to welcome the fact that our islands have better opportunities to attract tourists and that both Danish and foreign tourists have better opportunities and that it is possible to visit the unique gems that our islands make up.

But with this opportunity, where you can expect tourists all year round, it is also important that you are ready to accept them all year round. So if people come over to an island in November and the inn is closed and the merchant is closed and there is no place they can go to eat, they won't come back, and therefore it is really important that the islands also fill out the framework that we are giving them here today. We certainly welcome the results that have been achieved so far, and we look forward very much to seeing what this can create for the future. In the Left, we believe that it is appropriate to increase the subsidy for the reduction of ferry charges for non-governmental ferry services, so that the subsidy will have the optimal effect. The draft law thus reduces traffic inequality by extending the principle of road transport. The purpose of the draft law is also to change the period of application of the grant so that the municipalities will be able to benefit from the grant throughout the year, with the exception of the high season, rather than being able to use it only for a shorter part of the year. In this way, we are more supportive of the positive development of the small islands, which is why the Left naturally supports L 197, in other words this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504104346	The debate is open, and that is Mr. Male Larsen, the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20170504104409	Thank you. As our rapporteur unfortunately cannot be present in the Chamber today, I have been given the task of reading the rapporteur's speech. This bill follows up on the political agreement on party support rules reached between the government and the Social Democratic Party back in February. With the political agreement, we have followed up some of the recommendations made by the Party Support Committee and the Council of Europe's GRECO agency GRACO in recent years. The agreement therefore ensures more openness and transparency in the support to Danish parties from private individuals, companies and organisations. We are satisfied with this by the Social Democrats, and that was also the reason why we set up the Party Support Committee in the former SR government at the time. With the agreement and this legislative proposal, we ensure that private contributions are made transparent to all levels of the parties and to individual candidates. Today, only the parties' national organisations are required to publish private contributions of more than DKK 20,000. It will also apply in the future at local and regional level, and candidates for all elections will be subject to reporting and publication obligations. In addition, parties at all levels are prohibited from receiving anonymous grants of more than DKr 20,000. If a party receives an anonymous grant over DKK 20,000, the grant must be returned to the grant provider or alternatively transferred to an account with the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Ministry of the Interior. A guide is being prepared from the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Interior on what is perceived as subsidies, so that we also have clarity in this area. Finally, the draft law will introduce an obligation to audit the accounts of the parties' national organisations, so that the accounts of political parties which have been drawn up for the last parliamentary elections or European Parliament elections will in future be audited by an auditor approved by the statutory auditor. With the agreement, therefore, we are taking a step in the right direction. Some may say that we could have gone further, but it is always a balance between creating greater openness without imposing unnecessary bureaucracy on our party organisations. Thank you for the floor.

It is an agreement that follows up some of the recommendations made by the GRECO GRECO committee and some of the recommendations made by the Party Support Committee. Those rules apply today to the parties' national organisations, and we believe that there is reason to ensure that greater private contributions are made at all levels of the parties. We also think it is logical that in future the limit should be set at a price level to follow the general trend in wages. On the basis of this, the Left supports the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504110602	In January, the government and the Social Democratic Party reached an agreement to amend party support rules. The agreement contains a few good measures, but it does not address at all the problems that have been criticised internationally as well as by the Party Support Committee set up by the former government, the SR government. It is so unfortunate that we have missed the opportunity to do things properly when we finally come to an agreement and a legislative proposal to deal with the enormous secrecy of party support. Over and over Denmark has been criticized for its closeness to the cash flows in Danish politics. We have been criticised by the European Union, the Council of Europe and the Council of Europe's anti-corruption unit GRECO for our extreme secrecy about the economic interests hidden behind politicians. We are at a very low level when it comes to the ability of the people to see through what economic interests lie behind politicians. We did that before this deal, and we do that after this deal. I would also like to say something about the process leading up to the agreement, because I find it very strange that not all parties in the Folketing have been invited to negotiate on how to follow up the recommendations of the Party Support Committee. Why has it been so closed down a debate that has only included government parties and social democracy? It certainly looks like a horse trade, which means that the parties with the greatest interest in maintaining the secrecy of party support have joined forces and tightened up some rules that benefit themselves. In this house, we should have nothing to hide from the people who voted for us. If we have anything to hide from our constituents, the alarm bells have to ring. They have been beeping loudly for years when it comes to party support; when it comes to who sponsors the parties' and candidates' campaigns, because far too many parties have had too much to hide. The electorate and the public are entitled to know what financial interests are behind the money sent to politicians sponsoring parties and candidates' campaigns. We all have a responsibility in here to create confidence in the political system in order to avoid suspicion of corruption. When voters have to vote when deciding who should have power, they have to do so on an enlightened basis. Then they have the right to know who has paid thousands or even millions of dollars to the various parties. One of the greatest criticisms in the trade between government and social democracy is that it does not solve the problem of so-called collecting societies or business clubs or money clubs. It is collection associations in which companies and other donors can retain their anonymity, even if they send large sums of money to politicians and parties. In the money clubs you can bypass the rules of openness that exist. That is precisely why money clubs have been criticized over and over again. Why is nothing being done about the money clubs in the agreement that is now in place? One suspects that this is due to the fact that the parties behind the agreement have too much to lose; that they fear that financial support is becoming less and less; that sponsors get cold feet if the Danish people are told who they are. But there is also reason to rejoice today. That is because a very large minority has made an agreement in which we commit ourselves and each other to implementing the recommendations of the Party Support Committee when we get the majority to do so. We will lower the limit on how much money parties and politicians can receive anonymously. We will ensure that the donor's name and the actual and full amount are published as soon as it exceeds a limit of DKK 10,000. These requirements must include not only aid in kroner and ear, but also indirect aid in the form of, for example: campaign bets. At the same time, we want to put an end to the money clubs which have allowed parties and politicians with large sums of money to be supported for a number of years and yet remain anonymous. But I think it's too bad we have to wait. I would therefore like to call on the government, and in particular the Minister of the Liberal Alliance, whom we have been standing with shoulder to shoulder in the past to fight for more openness in politics, to make a new agreement, to invite us all, all parties of the Folketing, to negotiate to meet the criticism that has been voiced for years, and instead to agree on rules for party support that ensure transparency, because we believe that we should do so out of respect for the Danish people. They need to be able to look at us in the cards. And we must do so, not least in respect of the democracy that we should stand together to watch out for.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504112358	The alternative is a value-based party. We are based on six values, and one of them is transparency. I do not think transparency is so easy and good today. So I think there are a few reasons why we should cover up the amount that people pay in party support. And that is what we are discussing today, that is, the law on private contributions to political parties, the publication of political parties' accounts and the law on financial support for political parties, etc. It can surely be said that this is a very long-awaited proposal. In 2015, the Party Support Committee made its recommendations in a report. They propose a number of things that are directly to implement. However, these are not the recommendations proposed by the government. Instead, a number of things are being proposed which are both positive and positive. that the rules for transparency of contributions of more than DKr 20,000. In regional and local politics, the same requirements apply to land policy; anonymous grants of more than DKr 20,000. be prohibited; the country organisations of the parties shall be subject to an audit obligation. These are all positive things which are a step in the right direction, but we think that this is too small a step. Denmark has received criticism of the Council of Europe's anti-corruption unit GRECO, because there is far too little openness about party support in Denmark. In fact, we are in the category of globally unsatisfactory, which is the hardest criticism we can get. After all, it creates distrust of the political system, and no one knows whether a party or individuals allow their political opinions and decisions to be influenced by contributors with large purses. We do not know if this is happening in Denmark. But the possibility is there because in this area it's so incredibly closed. We believe that this is a major problem. Transparency about the cash flows in politics is essential for a well-functioning democracy. That is why we believe that at least the recommendations of the Party Support Committee should be introduced and, as I have said, they are straightforward to implement. So it is quite a mystery why the government and the Social Democrats will not do so. In other words, the recommendations of the party support committee go, among other things. to the effect that the limit on the time when contributions may be secret will be reduced from DKK 20,000. 10,000 kroner.; the exact amount of the total private contribution from each donor is published; that we are getting rid of the so-called'money clubs', i.e. money clubs that allow people and companies that are particularly generous towards some parties to stay completely out of the spotlight with what we think is legitimate creative technical manoeuvres and much more. We are of the opinion that Danish democracy deserves real transparency about the cash flows in Danish politics. We are therefore very pleased that we have been able to shake hands with the Enhedslisten, the Danish People's Party, the Radical Left and the Socialist People's Party that we will introduce the recommendations of the Party Support Committee on the day we get a majority. And I look forward very much to it when I or any other rapporteur for the alternative, in a future progressive majority, can take up this platform and, together with other rapporteurs, proclaim that Parliament intends to introduce the recommendations of the Party Support Committee. In the meantime, we are voting in favour of this bill, which everything else will be a small step in the right direction. But I hope that the government and the Social Democrats will change their attitude so that together we can ensure much more openness in Danish politics than this bill will create.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504114359	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it's the Minister for Economic Affairs and the Interior. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170504114410	I would like to start by thanking you for a good debate, which has, of course, come down to many details on the issue of private contributions to political parties. I understand that, in general terms, there is a positive indication of the draft law. I do not quite have an overview of whether it is all parties that vote in favour, but it seems that we are very close in any case, and as a minister we can only be happy about that. The bill we are discussing here results in a political agreement that has been concluded between the Tripartite Government and the Social Democratic Party and which requires a change of law. The draft law thus translates four elements of the agreement. Firstly, transparency of contributions to all levels of the parties and to individual candidates; secondly, prohibition of anonymous contributions above the limit of DKK 20,000. for parties and candidate lists; third, a price adjustment of the limit of DKK 20,000.; and, fourthly, the obligation to audit the accounts of the parties' national organisations. The part of the political agreement that does not require amendment, the fifth element, will be implemented by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Interior, following the adoption of the bill, drawing up a guidance document setting out detailed guidelines on when aid is covered by the Party Support Act and the Party Accounts Act. After all, the party support area can share the waters, and it can at least lead to a major debate, but I think it is gratifying that in one of the least corrupt countries in the world we are now making more open party support legislation than has been the case so far, something that has been talked about for many years and something that has not been done about, but which is actually succeeding in doing something about. So I am glad that we have made this agreement, and I am glad that we will have more openness, and I am glad that it seems that it is even a very broad majority in the Folketing that supports the bill. ParlaMint-DK_20170504130055	Thank you. As our rapporteur, Magnus Heunicke, unfortunately cannot be present in the Chamber today, I should like to read out the rapporteur's speech. The draft law before us abolishes the right to vote in local, regional and European elections for foreigners, for example. is in Denmark on toiled stays or who are deported foreigners. Today, it is already the case that, for example, extradited foreigners do not have the right to vote in elections to the Folketing or in referendums. In this way, this proposal envisages streamlining electoral law in Denmark in this area. For example, a foreigner on tolerated stays can: be expelled from Denmark because of serious crimes or because it is estimated that the person is a threat to the security of the state. In this case, a foreigner on tolerated residence would most often only be in Denmark, as a repatriation can lead to inhumane treatment in the country of origin. Therefore, it is also a temporary stay, which is why the deported person has no future in Denmark. For the same reasons, therefore, it is natural that foreigners who are on toiled stay do not have an influence on elections to the municipal council, to the regional council or to the European Parliament, in Denmark. The same applies to exiled foreigners. These are foreigners who are serving their sentence or waiting for repatriation. That is why the deportee is still in the country, even though the deportee has in principle been expelled. Here again, it seems very simple that if you have been expelled from Denmark, the right to vote is removed. As a matter of principle, if you are deported or have no future in Denmark, you should not have any influence on the way in which society is organized. As mentioned earlier, this principle already applies to elections to the Folketing and referendums in Denmark, thus transferring the same basic view to other elections in the Danish context. We also support the rapid entry into force of the proposal, so that the proposal will apply from the next municipal and regional elections in November. It is in line with that principle and for the reasons given above that we in the Social Democratic Party support the proposal. Thank you.

Similarly, in some of the hearings, questions are raised as to whether this bill is compatible with human rights. Nor do we think that there is any proper clarification in the consultation note. That is why we simply think that it is too unprepared and that there are too many questions that are not answered, and these are questions of some very fundamental and important things. Therefore, we cannot support the legislative proposal and we must find out whether we should vote yellow because we think there is a lack of information or whether we will vote against it. We'll find out. At least we can't support the bill. It seems premature, and this is also seen in the reply from the National Police, where they say that they think it will be difficult for them to find out who will then have deprived the right to vote, because it is the police who hold the data. They are in doubt as to whether they can achieve it, and this shows that this will be rushed through in order to be tough on immigrants again. It may be that a little more time should have been devoted to resolving some of these issues; at least if you want radical support. So we don't support the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20170504132816	Thank you very much, sir. Andreas Steenberg. There are no brief comments and I would like to welcome Mrs Kirsten Normann Andersen as rapporteur for the Socialist People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20170504132825	Thank you, President. In the SF, we can easily understand the moral considerations in relation to this bill. We also understand the desire to deprive citizens of their democratic right to vote. But for SF, it is important that we in Denmark can continue to boast of being a democracy, and the right to vote is closely linked to freedom of expression, which we value very much. Denmark has the right to vote in local and regional councils when you are 18 years old and if you do not have Danish citizenship and have had a continuous residence in the municipality in question for the last 3 years. A foreigner on toiled residence, a expelled foreigner or administratively expelled foreigner in Denmark is in Denmark because he cannot be returned because the country of origin is uncertain. It may be because of torture or persecution or other, and the person concerned will therefore remain in Denmark until the return is safe. They are therefore also legally resident in Denmark, contrary to what the Ministry points out. You have a social security number as long as you are in Denmark and thus you also retain your right to vote. Some foreigners on tolerated residence, expelled foreigners and administratively expelled foreigners are, indeed, undesirable in Denmark because they have committed crime in Denmark. In Denmark, criminals were granted and sentenced to vote in 1915. That was over a hundred years ago, and should we now deprive criminals of their right to vote, as they were then? In that case, there must be a really good reason. The abolition of the electoral right of foreigners who have been held liable, expelled foreigners and administratively expelled foreigners would seem to be a breach of the United Nations Convention on Civil and Political Rights ratified by Denmark in 1968, and the European Convention on Human Rights was incorporated into Danish law in 1992. In SF we therefore value human rights and democracy. We want a detailed analysis of the access to restricting the right to vote for foreigners on tolerated residences, expelled aliens and administratively expelled aliens, as envisaged in the proposal, and this is in the light of Denmark's human rights obligations under national and EU law. Only when we have an analysis of it will we be able to take a real position on the proposal. In fact, I share the view of the Radicals that this proposal is very badly prepared. Until then, we intend to vote against.

Secondly, foreigners who have been expelled by judgment. So I think it's a duty to adopt this bill and that it's actually a loving duty to implement this bill, because it's obviously a bad idea that these people can vote in Denmark when the Danish state doesn't want them to stay here. However, there is no logic in a state governed by the rule of law that one's political views with regard to who should be included should have an impact on whether or not one supports the sanction. The proposal should be split up so that each element is dealt with separately, so that the parties have an opportunity to support the attempts with which they agree in principle and vote against the attempts with which they disagree. We have said this every time we deal with these mass weddings of free municipalities, but there has not been any consultation in the past, even by the previous government, and I have my doubts as to whether there will be this time. This will only have the consequence of voting against the draft law, as it contains a number of attempts which we disagree with in principle. There are also positive measures. If we look at that page, we think it is good to offer microloans for setting up our own business to selected unemployed people with skills and motivation for entrepreneurship. We have, for example. The Commission has already supported an independent legislative proposal on the same subject. It is also quite right to try out new ways of clarifying the situation of the child, the young person and the family, for setting objectives and objectives for action and follow-up in individual cases. The same applies to the possibility of derogating from the rules on a separate plan of support for parents during the placement of a child, but provided that the plan is instead part of a coherent plan for parents. It smells a bit of holistic processing, and it's an old proposal from Enhedslisten, so we like that. On the negative side, there are a number of totally unacceptable proposals. In the area of children, as I said, it is fine to try to gear the system better to some holistic procedure, but we are very, very concerned about the derogation of section 50 studies in the light of the many scandals of child failure that we have seen in recent years. So we cannot support that part. We are very much opposed to opening the door to the forced relocation of people who have considerable and lasting disabilities without consent. We fully agree with Danske Handicaporganisations' criticism of this point. We are also opposed to weakening the supervision of nursing homes in the Services Act, so that notified supervision can replace unannounced supervision. It is quite unacceptable to introduce the possibility of purchase services for practical assistance and personal care and rehabilitation in connection with disability and purchase benefits in relation to social pedagogy assistance. Buying services are just a fine word for user charges, and it does not belong in a welfare state, which is to ensure that all citizens receive the necessary help regardless of personal economy. At the same time, as the Danish Handicap Federation points out, there is no limit to what services can be purchased within the provisions referred to in the Services Act. After all, it opens a barn door for municipalities to lower quality standards and to speculate that then the citizens who can afford can simply buy the services that have been cut away themselves. We already have the infamous Køgedom from 2012 which said that the municipality could reduce or completely deprive the citizen of practical assistance and personal care solely on the basis of a lowered quality standard, a quality standard that had been lowered as a result of savings requirements in the municipality. It is also wrong to relax the requirement of fairness in company practice for active foreigners. The rules on protection against abuse of internship and wage subsidies are already far too weak, as can be seen, especially in the area of refugees, so that the rules on protection must be tightened, not weakened. It is also unacceptable to prolong the current experiments in the field of employment according to the consultation responses of the LO and DA. We fully agree with the LO that there is no need to extend the experiment with a higher hourly limit for voluntary unpaid work. Moreover, we see no reason for the general extension of experiments proposed in several areas of law. An experiment is an experiment, and when the expiry date is exceeded, it will stop unless there is an exceptionally good, objective reason to extend it, and there is no such thing in these cases, because it is almost a rubber stamp. Our conclusion is that several of the attempts threaten the legal certainty of the citizen and their claim to a proper service. These parts of the experiments are clearly driven by austerity considerations, i.e. general economic pressure on the municipalities, so the arrow ultimately points to the majority in this Chamber who has for several years tightened the screw over the economy of the municipalities, and the funny thing is that it is the same majority that will then actually use it as a basis for introducing experiments which both undermine the legal certainty of the citizen and lead to a high risk of general service degradation.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504143939	The debate is open, and I would like to welcome Mr. Orla Sea from the Social Democratic Party, the first rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20170504144004	The draft law we are discussing here has been tabled in response to a request from the Tax Council, and in this respect it is so much more over in the technical part that we are in the process of drafting the legislation. The reason for this is that the Tax Council has pointed out that there are employees who, in some cases only by acquiring a small part of the employer's business, are considered to be self-employed. And in this way they will benefit from the low company tax, which is currently 22 per cent, even though they are actually employees. Our industry must undoubtedly have the best opportunities for growth, for development and for creating jobs, but we cannot have systems, as in this case the business system, being used for their purposes. So the intention is to ensure that employees employed in a so-called tax transparent company, for example. a partnership or a limited partnership is not regarded as an independent economic operator and obtains a tax advantage simply by acquiring a very small share of the company. We think it is obvious that if existing rules are applied for their purpose, we must ensure that they are put to an end. If there is an unintended use of the rules and a tax idea that is politically opposed, yes, we must take action, as is the case with the proposal here. That is why it is quite appropriate for the proposal to be put forward and, on the part of the Socialist Group, we support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504144618	Thank you, sir. Dennis Flydtkjær. The next rapporteur is Mrs Louise Schack Elholm from the Left, the Liberal Party of Denmark.
ParlaMint-DK_20170504144625	Thank you, and thank you for the floor. This bill seeks to change the assessment of what is characterised as a self-employed activity. The legislation is based on a recommendation from a unanimous Tax Council which, on the basis of a specific case, thought that practice was changed and went too far. The government has a strong focus on the fact that the independent entrepreneurs in Denmark have good conditions which allow them to consolidate and thus make greater investments. In this way, they have roughly the same conditions as if the company were operating in a share or equity company. The company scheme established a system to ensure that this is the case. However, it must not be the case that the low company tax regime must be capable of being abused by persons who are not really independent operators. Unfortunately, it has happened that in some cases employees could only take advantage of the advantages of self-employment by acquiring a very small part of the employer's business. We believe that this is a gap and we believe that we should close it. Income from wages must be taxed as wage income, not as income from business and therefore it is proposed that it should be separated more clearly when you have fiscal status as an employee and when you are self-employed. The assessment of whether an activity is self-employed must be made on the basis of a concrete assessment of all factors such as the level of economic risk and the influence on the undertaking. In order to benefit from the tax advantages provided by the corporate scheme, it must not be enough to have only bought a small share of a company. If, on the basis of a specific assessment, one is considered to be an employee, one should not be able to apply the same tax conditions as are calculated for self-employed persons. On behalf of the Left, I have the honour to inform you that the Left supports the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504150317	Thank you. Then it is my pleasure to welcome the country's treasure minister on the platform.
ParlaMint-DK_20170504150330	I would like to start by thanking you for the receipt of the bill and for the debate it has given rise to. The draft law, as a number of rapporteurs have also mentioned, is an example of the need to find pragmatic solutions when we want to take account of economic operators, while at the same time ensuring that the rules are not watered down or exploited, if you like. The aim of the draft law is to ensure that employees are treated as employees and traders as traders for tax purposes, or quite simply that wages are taxed as wages. The Tax Council has in a call to me from 2015, so it is a while ago, warned against an unfortunate practice which means that employees would be to consider as entrepreneurs by just acquiring a quite small, some would say a microscopic shareholding in the company in which they are employed when, for example, it. is organised as a partnership. It has thus become apparent that, according to the tax practice in force, it is possible, through special company structures, to allow employees to be regarded as self-employed if they are, in fact, not self-employed but employees. Why, then, do some people want to take advantage of that opportunity? This is because employees can use the enterprise system in this way and save their wages for a temporary low tax rate of 22 per cent. instead of paying normal income tax and possibly • I think that will have been the case in many of these cases • top tax on the salary. I have no desire to make it harder for independent traders to run their business, but when a unanimous Tax Council sends what is called an early warning, an early warning, to me as tax minister, I have to find a solution to deal with the problem they point out. In fact, we have spent some time there precisely in order to ensure what some rapporteurs are asking for, namely that this should be a targeted intervention. The Tax Council also asked that it should be a targeted intervention and not something that is too broad, and it has taken some time to make the model that the Danish Parliament is now examining. We have very reasonable and sensible rules in the company system, and it is important to safeguard them and, moreover, to limit them to the fact that only those who are genuine operators can avail themselves of these rules. In fact, the business system allows the self-employed to consolidate and save in the company for this temporary low tax, which we have chosen for the time being to give to people who are self-employed, also because there are risks of being self-employed as opposed to being employed. Thus, the self-employed person is not required to pay full tax on the company's profits until the owner withdraws money. Of course, when you withdraw the money, you have to pay taxes. That goes without saying. And if we are not to undermine the business system, it should be reserved for the real operators. It is only in this way that it can be defended and safeguarded. The draft law should no longer be the sole determining factor for whether one is considered to be self-employed in a partnership, that the income recipient owns a share of the enterprise. This has been the case in the past, i.e. that you owned a share of the company and that it was the only thing you looked at. It is now necessary to look in the future at the specific circumstances in order to assess whether the person concerned is an economic operator, and in particular the extent of the individual's specific risk and influence on businesses. If this assessment results in being considered as an economic operator, the enterprise scheme can be used • it is only fair and fair but if the assessment results in being regarded as an employee, the income of the work must be taxed as wage. This has been criticised by several quarters, and a number of rapporteurs have also mentioned this, among others. The rapporteur of the Danish People's Party states that it is difficult to determine when there is an employee relationship and when there is a self-employed activity. To that I must say that I understand that criticism. I think it is perfectly legitimate to travel, and we must do what we can in committee to address the criticisms which, among other things, have been voiced. Mr. Dennis Flydtkjær, for I myself, both as tax minister, as a citizen and as a member of parliament, are in favour of the fact that a law is very clear about what is applicable. Unfortunately, when it is a business activity, it is not clearly defined it is the challenge here but depends on a number of specific circumstances. A very simple rule as to when the only decisive thing is whether you own a share of a company or not, regardless of how small that share of and what the conditions are, with that letter and the early warning, that is, the early warning that I have received from the Tax Council, has proved untenable. I am very happy to listen to other suggestions on how to address this, and we have also been in dialogue in the Treasury with some of those who have sent in hearings to find out if there is another way forward to narrow it down here. At this stage, it turns out to be difficult. That is why, with what is in the draft law, we are proposing, in my opinion, that in the vast majority of cases there will be no doubt as to whether you are an employee or a self-employed person. This is not a new definition, because there is already a very wide-ranging practice through decisions and judgments which deal precisely with the distinction between employees and self-employed persons, and that practice can also be applied because it is as valid as it was before, even after the adoption of the bill. In order to respond to the concerns expressed, the comments on the provision will be expanded so as to specify more clearly the specific proposals to be emphasised, i.e. those described in the draft law. And as I mentioned earlier, this proposal has come on the basis of a warning from a General Tax Council, and it is a warning which I, as Minister of Taxation, find very difficult to ignore. It is so clear to me that, with this bill, we have made an intervention that is as targeted as possible. As I said, it was also a request from the Tax Council. However, in relation to the concern raised by the Danish People's Party, I am of course constructively committed to trying to clarify this in committee.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504170907	First of all, I would like to thank seven out of nine rapporteurs for a positive reception of the draft law, and I would also like to thank the last two for having taken on the role of the devil's lawyer in this matter. As a journalist, I know that it is also important to ask questions on quite obvious matters, so that there is an opportunity to argue on the matter, and there is Mr. Henning Hylllested and Mr. Andreas Steenberg played the devil's lawyer here, and that's really good. Even a criminal is also entitled to a defence lawyer, you might say, and a bad law may also have the right to defence lawyers here in the Folketing. The aim of the bill is to make it easier for older people in Denmark to have a driving licence without compromising road safety. As the rules are today, all driving licences are issued to older people over 73 years of age with a shorter period of validity than the normal 15 years. This shorter period of validity requires the elderly to renew driving licences more frequently than other age groups, and they must undergo medical screening for each renewal. This general age screening is carried out to intercept the elderly who should no longer drive, for example due to dementia. However, a review of the large amount of research in this area shows that there is no evidence that this age screening has a positive impact on road safety, and screening only for very few older people means that driving licences are not renewed. On the other hand, screening is very burdensome for the elderly, which are estimated to cost between 100 million. kr. and 150 million kr. each year for renewal. In addition, for many of the elderly, the screening is seen as being offensive to those who are fresh enough to continue driving and those who know when it is time to stop driving. The screening system has also shown in several cases a negative impact on the road safety of older people, as more elderly people stop driving before they need it because they do not want to try to renew their driving licence. Instead, older people become vulnerable road users, such as pedestrians or cyclists, where older people are a particularly vulnerable group. Against this background, I do not believe that it is still sensible to maintain a system in which older people have to renew driving licences more often than others. On the other hand, as they do today, doctors need to sharpen their attention to the elderly, who should not continue driving. I therefore propose that we remove the rules of the Road Traffic Code that driving licences for the elderly over 75 years are issued for a shorter period of validity. I also propose that the authority to allow the Minister for Transport, Building and Housing to lay down rules whereby driving licences for the elderly over 73 years may be issued for a shorter period of validity should be abolished. I look forward to further consideration here in the Folketing, and I am of course available to answer any questions that may arise in this regard. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504171704	First of all, I would like to thank the Minister for taking the initiative in this debate on the statement. This is a good and much needed opportunity to highlight some of the challenges facing our rural areas. Fortunately, we are experiencing positive developments in several parts of the country, as the Minister also mentioned in his written statement, but we are far from achieving it. There are still too many places where they are experiencing a decline in the population, have high unemployment rates and where a large proportion of citizens are on cash benefits and early retirement. Many feel that they are coupled by developments. It may be the local doctor, primary school or supermarket that is closed or moved. However, I would like to start by recognising some of the initiatives taken by the government. This applies, for example, to the relocation of state jobs, the agreement between the parties of the Danish Parliament to ensure better medical coverage throughout the country and cheaper ferry tariffs, although we are not in line with the promises made before the elections, about the introduction of the full road model. However, these are good proposals that the Social Democrats support because they help to move in the right direction in order to ensure a better balance for a country. But that is only one side of history, because history also includes the fact that when the government conducts economic distribution policies, it is unfortunately all too often in the wrong direction. It is not only social but also geographical. This is true when it comes to distributing money. We know that the Government's desire for top tax concessions will benefit the municipalities of Northern Zealand in particular, while rural areas are being neglected. For example, a citizen of Hørsholm with the government's previous 2025 plan would receive a top tax relief of DKK 2,500. a year, while the top tax relief on Langeland would be only 40 DKK. We would also have seen the same picture with housing taxation if the Social Democrats had not succeeded in introducing the so-called millionaire knuckle. But it is not only when it comes to distributing money that there are challenges, because the government's savings knife often hits rural areas. This applies, among other things, to: the annual savings in education, which hit the education institutions in the province and rural areas particularly hard, because they have smaller teams and educational institutions that are particularly vulnerable to cuts. And LAG funds, which otherwise contribute to growth and development and employment in rural areas, are cut by one third. Then there is the cash aid ceiling that hits municipalities in rural areas with many on transfer income, such as Lolland Municipality, really hard. The municipality even finds that several cash benefits recipients move to the municipality as a result of the ceiling on cash benefits in order to obtain a cheaper accommodation. There are many of us who believe that the new ceiling on cash benefits is giving impetus to a development in which cash benefits recipients are looking for outlying areas where it is already difficult to find jobs. It is a source of inequality in economic and geographical terms. The population's settlement patterns are changing and inequality is increasing. Instead of getting jobs, there will be cash benefits recipients. For example, there is Lolland, who peaked the list of municipalities with the most citizens on cash benefits in 2014. It shows figures from Danmarks Statistik. In 1990 Lolland looked like an average municipality in terms of population composition, but high housing prices in the metropolitan area have led many citizens on the edge or outside the labour market to pack the moving boxes to settle on the island. It is a development which simply reinforces the ceiling on cash benefits. In addition, the government continues to oppose the Togfonden DK, which otherwise aims to bind the whole country together, just as the government has tried to tighten the ground for new offshore wind turbines, even if it would have cost private jobs in rural areas. It is therefore surprising that none of the so-called outermost municipalities received funding from the broadband pool in 2016, while projects in Greater Copenhagen have been supported in return. We assumed otherwise that the purpose of the pool was to support rural areas with poor broadband coverage. Last month it emerged that no meetings of the Government's Advisory Committee on Rural Development have been held since the change of government. Both the Rural Councils, Agriculture & Food, the Free Air Council and the Cooperative Merchants are among the represented. The examples I have mentioned here, which help to pull the country off the ground, show that the government could well use a few good advices. My advice to the government must therefore be that a meeting should be convened as soon as possible and that organizations should be consulted on the policy pursued. We need to create a better-balanced country. We need to restore security, development and cohesion throughout the country. On the social democratic side, we have therefore proposed the following initiatives to create balance and growth throughout Denmark: the relocation of state jobs must be completed and new relocation opportunities must be assessed on an ongoing basis. Specifically, we will strengthen the SKAT with 1,000 additional employees in four tax centres outside the major cities. We suggest that two are located in Jutland, one is located on Funen and one is located on Zealand and Lolland-Falster. Together with SF, we have pointed to an experiment with local service centres with functions such as job center, library, community service, medical delivery and health clinics, which must be open for a few days a week because it can help restore local welfare services. We maintain our ambition for the Train Fund DK, which binds the country together and upgrades 11 regional sections. With our latest growth initiative, we propose that the Growth Fund be strengthened by 5 billion. kr., so as to improve access to risk capital for small and medium-sized enterprises. This will benefit rural businesses in particular. Finally, we must ensure education throughout Denmark by giving unskilled people a training boost and canceling the government's planned savings on vocational training. Many people find that the distance between country and city has increased, but Denmark is too small a country for big differences. We therefore hope that, in future, we in the Danish Parliament can agree to remedy the imbalances that have arisen between the country and the city in recent years. Indeed, there is a great potential for growth and employment in our rural areas. That potential must be provided with the right political initiatives and in cooperation with all the people and fire-souls living in rural areas, the people who are passionate about their territories and have the resources to lift them. We must act, and we must not slow down. Let me give you a current example where the government should be careful not to sit on its hands, but should be more active. Maabjerg Energy Concept will be the first in Denmark to establish full-scale production in Holstebro of second generation bioaetanol based on straw, and more than 2 billion will be invested. kr. in the project, which has already been promised 293 million. kr. Union support. It is estimated that the project here can create 1,000 jobs in Holstebro and throughout northwest Jutland. In order to get started, the political framework is lacking, and here is where the government is slowing down. We in the Social Democratic Party have pressed for progress on the project and, above all, the creation of the many jobs, and now it is up to the government to clarify what specific conditions should be laid down. ParlaMint-DK_20170504174905	It has been a condition for many years that there has been a migration from our rural areas and outlying areas to the cities. This is, and we all know it, a global trend that we see there, but it is and remains the main problem for our rural areas, our islands and, in general, for our outermost regions. I would say that in Denmark, in particular, it was pushed up through the 00s through the very large reforms of centralisation that were adopted at the time. It was, of course, first and foremost the structural reform, which greatly reduced the number of municipalities and counties, which then became regions, reduced the number of state assemblies from 15 to 5 state administrations and centralised tax centres. In addition, the judicial reform and police reform also contributed to centralising state jobs in the larger cities. With these reforms carried out by the Left and the Conservatives and supported by the Danish People's Party, with the support of changing party constellations to the concrete proposals, the distance from citizens, especially in rural and peripheral areas, to politicians suddenly became very large. The number of elected local politicians in the city and county councils decreased dramatically and, as I said, many public jobs disappeared from the outermost regions. I therefore find it hypocritical that the very same parties, particularly in recent years, have been vying to appear as the saviors of the outskirtsdanmark, and it is not least with regard to the relocation of state jobs that we have already mentioned. But there, as in the case of the chest, it is hypocritical, in my opinion, when the truth is that we are clapping on a development which we ourselves have pushed quite violently. And when there is no local primary school, you cannot draw children's families, the merchant closes down, and the fire souls, which are crucial to the development and life of a village, also disappear. It is a negative downward spiral that is thereby created. It is, of course, gratifying to see that our outermost regions also benefit from the fact that we are on our way out of the crisis. In fact, the population has increased in some of the places that previously showed a fall, and employment has actually gone the same way. Knowing that LAG funds generate 3-4 times as much money from funds and local authorities and result in the creation of hundreds of local jobs, frankly, we think it is a little crazy to cut the economically very modest effort. In the report, the government mentions that the so-called modernisation of the planning law, as it is called, must promote tourism and, up to 2025, must provide one third more tourists and a turnover in tourism of 140 billion. kr. In the Enhedslisten we are of the opinion that, to a certain extent, we are shooting ourselves in the foot when we want to give the municipalities greater access to establishing projects and cottage building in the coastal zone and even give rise to projects within the beach protection line. In fact, it is on nature experiences that we in Denmark score high on, let's call it the tourism barometer, and here it is not least our freely accessible, open and pristine shores and beaches that make the impact for both foreign and Danish tourists. Moreover, it is worth noting that tourism has taken a huge leap up again after some sluggish years after the crisis, and perhaps we should actually put cold water into this industry's outlook. They're probably not as gloomy as it looked right after the crisis. As far as the relocation of state jobs is concerned, this is an initiative that we supported on the part of the Enhedslisten, but at the same time we have criticised the concrete plan for relocation as being too premature and poorly worked out and analysed. For the same reason, the plan is underfunded, which means that the relevant agencies and councils etc. I have to pay for a large part of the relocation myself. At the same time, relocation has led to significantly increasing processing times in some agencies, but the government is consoling itself with the fact that it will probably be better when the relocation has fallen into place, and it is to be hoped that it will. But if the situation had now been thoroughly analysed prior to the relocation, and had been tried to provide some good incentives for the former employees in terms of job guarantees, probation periods, pay supplements, or whatever it might be thought of to move with, it could have been that some more people had moved with and thus retained their competence and knowledge and thus avoided the loss of knowledge that has come out of it in some places, at least. We are absolutely opposed to the experimental scheme referred to in the statement on which the government is on its way with regard to an announced dialogue with the European Commission that foreigners can acquire summer homes in specially selected areas. We simply believe that it will be a slippery slope and that the European Union will use such a scheme to lift the summer house rule completely in the long term, and that is something we are strongly opposed to in the Enhedslisten. This will simply mean that holiday homes, especially along the west coast of Jutland, but certainly elsewhere, will rise greatly in value and will not be able to get close to ordinary Danes. At the same time, it would mean that the summer houses would seem to be more or less empty for most of the year, since you wouldn't take a vacation in a summer house if you live in southern Germany, for example. I must also ask: is there even a majority in favour of this proposal? It was a rather rhetorical question which was also addressed to the Danish People's Party. The government has so far sounded pretty sure that it is not a problem, and we hope that is true, but it has to come to a test, to put it plainly. In the Enhedslisten, we want to help develop our outermost regions, rural areas and islands. I am not going to mention all ten points, just a few of them, which we believe are of the greatest importance: we attach great importance to building local councils around with not only the right of consultation, but also the right of initiative, decision-making powers and the economy, and I would very much like to highlight here what is happening in the established rural committee in Sønderborg Municipality, where village guilders have really managed to get in and where they actually have real competence and, so to speak, their own economy to work with. Whether it should be through a state bank or in other ways, we have not just made such a determination, but we believe that there is still a problem, even though the crisis has done its work.

Danish rural areas offer plenty of opportunities for nature experiences, organic food production, culture and strong binding communities, opportunities for Danish family life, opportunities for green conversion and opportunities for entrepreneurship. At the same time, we are going to learn how to think in completely new forms of living, living and working. In this context, we are delighted in the alternative that the government has taken a couple of important steps towards nuanced perceptions of Danish rural areas. Last year, the government described cities as growth locomotives that drive development in the rest of the country. I am glad that in the last statement we are trying to go beyond that. Now I'm saying "we" as if I'm part of the government, so I'd rather say that the government goes beyond this contradiction between country and city. We must get away from the idea that growth belongs only to urban areas. Instead, we must regard rural areas as a good investment rather than merely talking about sources of high costs and that it is expensive to offer education and run schools and everything else. In Denmark, we have in recent years defined rural policy as a citizen-driven policy. There we fully agree with the alternative that it makes sense. This is also how we have developed our policy in this area over the last two years. We'll get out soon enough. I'm here with the entire catalogue. But at the same time, it must not be a disclaimer from in here. Danish rural policy has lacked a clear vision and political priority at national level. We are still experiencing it. Then we can discuss whether it is holistic and long-term enough. But the government has carried out much-needed and sensible measures. In the government's statement, I find that there is a focus on one-sided economic growth, in which the green transition is cut in particular. It is at the expense of social contexts; it is at the expense of nature and the environment. For example, the government mentions restrictions on agricultural growth opportunities as the first challenge. In the alternative, we believe that we should have an eye on the economic opportunities of green businesses, and we believe that Danish rural areas have an important role to play in the green economy. The European Union says that we have 20 million jobs in the green economy in the next few years. It is not because we want to under-priority agriculture, but instead we see that the growth of the future and the creation of jobs in agriculture are taking place in the context of ecological change. Similarly, it is vital for us to ensure geographical coverage of training provision. There must therefore be room for other perspectives than the narrow focus that we are currently experiencing on business interests. In the alternative, therefore, we want growth which takes account of the link between the economy, social relations, nature and the environment. And we do not believe that this is done by focusing narrowly on a relaxation of the law on plans or by pushing nature into the implementation of the agricultural package. Instead, we need forward-looking strategies. For example, tourism has great potential by encouraging people to enjoy the wild nature and the true nature experiences. We therefore need more wild nature and untouched forest, biodiversity in local plans. Finally, let us focus on the violations that are taking place in our nature and environment, with some nature officers. I hope that the citizens-driven policy that we come out with can inspire and encourage dialogue on future solutions. It therefore seems somewhat contradictory that the government is maintaining its savings on LAG funds. It is funds that are directly targeted at business projects and jobs. The Enhedslisten's rapporteur covered this very well in terms of the quality and results of the funds. The funds guarantee, among other things. these anchorages in local fiery souls that are vital to our rural areas. For us, a balanced Denmark is a more rich and diverse Denmark. I am convinced that we must make far greater investments than we do today. In the alternative, we also attach great importance to the fact that these investments are in local solutions and in local communities, by engaging citizens when they are part of a binding community. Today we see an incredible number of initiatives in which civil society, local councils, cultural life, fire souls, organisations and municipalities organise themselves in new forms of cooperation and address the completely local challenges and develop new solutions that we may also be inspired by in cities. If Danish rural policy is to succeed, then it requires that our municipalities also act as facilitators in this strengthened interaction between authorities, civil society, associations and citizens. And here we need much greater and experimental freedom for the municipalities to free citizens in the new forms of cooperation. To conclude, I would like to stress the importance of geography as a political dividing line and of the fact that the challenges facing rural areas are the challenges of Denmark as a whole. This applies, for example, to the geographical inequality we see in access to health and the length of our citizens' lives. It is deeply worrying that a man on Lolland lives 12 years shorter than a man in North Zealand. So this illustrates the importance of improving the thinking of the geographical issue in the Folketing when we develop policy in all areas: education, social affairs, taxation, etc. This requires that we weigh the social and environmental consequences as much as the economic ones when we take these major public decisions. So the economic recovery has not automatically benefited rural areas. This means that an active political effort is needed to achieve positive rural development. I expect the municipalities to use the new planning law as an effective tool to make it easier to operate, work and live in all parts of the country. Both employment, population and housing markets are developing positively. A few years ago, employment fell. It is now replaced by increases in most municipalities. For example: many Sjællandske municipalities have come along very well. There are still areas of declining employment. This applies to North- and North-West Jutland, Southern Denmark and the islands. But there will be fewer of those areas. The population has also begun to grow in most municipalities. This is particularly clear on Zealand and Funen. However, parts of northern and western Jutland, Southern Denmark and the islands continue to be marked by a declining population, but the decline is less than before. In recent years the housing market has seen a growing number of empty buildings, farm buildings, schools and commercial buildings etc. outside the major cities. The background is, among other things, that agriculture is combined into larger and more efficient farms and a weak housing market and negative population development. The uninhabited homes are a challenge with major social consequences. The empty homes are damaging settlements and tourism and can help create a negative spiral. The largest share of uninhabitable dwellings is found in North and North West Jutland, South Denmark and the islands. However, it is gratifying that there is now an increase in the number of homes sold. All municipalities have seen an increase since 2013, most of them by more than 10%. At the same time, mortgage lending is now increasing in most municipalities, i.e. a further positive trend which suggests a development in the right direction. The Danish small islands often have more in common with each other than they have with the municipalities they are part of, and therefore they have their own section in the statement. Our small islands are particularly challenged in relation to the development that has been going on over a long period of time. The total population on the Danish small islands was pr. January 1, 2017 at 4,210, and that's a fall of 12%. Since 2008. At the same time, the population at national level has increased by almost 5%. There is therefore a major challenge and a need for conversion in the small islands. In addition, the small islands have considerably more older than the rest of the country, which are relatively few children and young people and many over 65 years of age. The small islands also differ from the rest of the country on a number of socio-economic key figures, as shown in the report, and therefore initiatives have been launched with the Government's initiative on growth and development throughout Denmark, which directly accommodates the islands. Among other things: It has become cheaper to get to and from the islands outside the summer holidays of schools, and in the years to come, millions more are being allocated for price reductions on ferry tickets. This is to improve conditions for the non-bridged islands, and it has already been a huge success on the islands. After six months of reduced prices, 17 out of 21 municipalities have reported progress in passenger numbers on local ferries. For example: the number of passengers in Læsø has increased by 30%, while the island's turnover as a result of the increase has increased by 9.2 million. kr. However, we have also launched a national tourism strategy. The strategy was published in September 2016 and most of its 66 initiatives have been initiated and continuously followed by the National Tourism Forum. We have also abolished the PRS tax. The total savings benefit both households and businesses. For companies, the final saving is estimated to be 3.8 billion. kr., which is primarily expected to benefit a number of Jutland municipalities and Kalundborg. Then we have decided to introduce a requirement for an explanation of significant rural consequences. This means that significant consequences for rural areas must be clearly reflected in legislative proposals put forward in the Folketing. One of the most significant of the overall initiatives is undoubtedly the modernisation of the Planning Act. The Plan Act provides a framework for development opportunities across the country and today presents a number of challenges for the municipalities, companies and citizens to create growth and development throughout Denmark. The government wants the law of plans to be a major tool for the development of the whole of Denmark, while preserving the environment and rich nature. The revised draft law amending the Schedule Act was at first reading in the Folketing on 2 February 2017. The bill is expected to be adopted before the summer break. The modernisation of the law on plans includes, inter alia: a relaxation of rules for redundant buildings and for the expansion of housing and small businesses in rural areas. •Cities of redevelopment are introduced as a new concept in municipal planning and allow municipalities to put in place more long-term and holistic plans and opportunities for coastal municipalities to designate development areas in the coastal area, where more access is given to planning for the development of towns, villages or businesses, and then there is also a more flexible framework for the use of holiday homes. All these measures must make it easier and more attractive to live and operate in rural areas, but they must also maintain life and jobs in rural areas. There must be good conditions for living both in the countryside and in the towns and cities, to have a good working life and family life and the security of the availability of public institutions. That is why a special committee for viable villages has been set up. The committee must focus on the future of villages and how they can be viable and make concrete recommendations for conversion and renewal towards viable villages, thereby contributing to the government's ambition for growth and development throughout Denmark. We will also repeal rules on the validity of driving licences for the elderly. The aim is to increase the mobility of older people and to help older people, including the elderly in rural areas, remain self-reliant for a long time. The requirement ensures that almost 2200 households, businesses and holiday homes spread over 245 areas have access to a mobile broadband connection with a download speed of at least 30 Mb/s, an upload speed of at least 3 Mb/s and voice coverage. In the short term there will be temporary costs of relocation itself, but in the longer term there are also expected to be economic gains for the state, for example. in the form of lower rental prices outside the metropolitan area and an optimisation of the building stock in the capital. ParlaMint-DK_20170504185218	Denmark's annual Union contribution shall be determined in accordance with the Regulation on the Union's own resources. This Regulation was adopted by all 28 Heads of State or Government of the European Union in 2013 in parallel with the adoption of the Agreement on the multiannual financial framework of the European Union for the period 2014-2020. The regulation on the EU's own resources has since been ratified by the parliaments of all 28 countries, and in the case of Denmark, the agreement was ratified by the Folketing on 19 December 2014. It must be understood that the motion for a resolution requires the Government to renegotiate an agreement reached unanimously among all the Heads of State or Government of the European Union and subsequently approved by the Danish Parliament. The aim of the proposal is to prevent additional payments to the EU and renegotiate Denmark's contribution to Britain's rebate. The government generally agrees that we must avoid additional costs to the European Union. This is also the approach to the debate on the EU's annual budgets, and the government acts in order to ensure an appropriate priority for EU budget resources and ensure as low expenditure as possible. In addition, it is the government's approach that Britain's withdrawal from the EU should not lead to increases in the EU budget and therefore not in Denmark's EU contribution. This view is also shared by our budget-responsible colleagues in other countries. However, the government does not agree that Denmark should make a wish to renegotiate the Danish EU contribution, and that is on two basic grounds. Firstly, most of the recitals and remarks in the motion for a resolution are misleading and erroneous. Secondly, Denmark is, after all, a responsible and credible country which accepts the agreements that have been concluded. In order to justify the proposal, it is stated, inter alia, that the Danish State has had an average net contribution of approximately 10.8 billion. kr. Regulation (EU) No 182/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 16 February 2011 laying down the rules and general principles concerning mechanisms for control by Member States of the Commission's exercise of implementing powers (OJ L 55, 28.2.2011, p. 13). However, it is not only true to look at the net contribution of the State, as this leaves out a significant contribution and repatriation of EU funds to municipalities, regions, private and self-owned institutions and organisations etc. I already have in an answer to parliamentary question No. 55 as from January this year, Denmark's net contribution to the EU, which, on the basis of the Commission's accounting figures, is estimated at an average of 6.4 billion. kr. Annually in the period 2006-2015. It is the Commission's accounting figures that are the current accounting method. It also states that the Commission's mid-term review proposal is likely to be adopted unchanged, which could increase Denmark's EU contribution by 4.1 billion. kr. Until 2020. Nor is that correct, as the expenditure in the Commission's proposal has been substantially reduced. This is due to a great deal of effort from Denmark and other countries responsible for the budget. The European Parliament's Committee on Foreign Affairs was informed back in December 2016 in connection with the presentation of the General Affairs Council. In addition, it should be mentioned in particular: Britain's withdrawal will certainly entail additional costs to be paid by the remaining 27 countries and that the financing key for this will be based on the current financing of Britain's EU rebate. It is not certain at all that Britain's withdrawal will entail additional expenditure to be covered by the EU-27. As mentioned, the clear approach taken by Denmark and other countries responsible for the budget is that Britain's withdrawal should not lead to increases in the EU contribution. Moreover, there has been no question whatsoever that the current approach to financing the UK's EU rebate should be used to finance a possible financing gap created by Britain's withdrawal. The EU's future level of expenditure and its financing will be discussed in the negotiation of the EU's next multiannual financial framework, which will apply from 2021. That agreement must also be adopted unanimously by all EU countries. In accordance with current practice, the government will of course obtain a negotiating mandate for this in the Parliamentary Committee on Europe. Even if the reasons for this proposal were correct, I do not think that anything good would come from Denmark's current desire to renegotiate the Danish EU contribution. As I said, both the current agreement on the EU's multiannual financial framework and the regulation on the EU's own resources have been established for a seven-year period, and all Member States have committed themselves to doing so. The agreements do not contain any provision allowing a Member State to re-open the agreements. This can only be done on a proposal from the Commission in specific cases. It is therefore not considered realistic that Denmark should renegotiate its EU contribution or the financing of Britain's rebate, as required by the motion for a resolution. Moreover, it will only weaken Denmark's credibility to suddenly start questioning the validity of agreements entered into, well to mark on a false basis. The government expects all partners to live up to agreements reached, and how could we expect this if we do not also acknowledge the agreements we are making ourselves? Against this background, the government cannot support the Danish People's Party's motion for a parliamentary resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504191322	Thank you. Let me start with the positive: in the Enhedslisten, we fully agree with the proposers that employees in Denmark pay too much, too much money for the European Union. But I think it is important to add that this is only half the truth, because the billions we pay and which we get again are not really the ones we can decide for ourselves. It is money that the European Union grants us for specific purposes. Let me take one example: of the large amount of money we are sending to the European Union, we will get a lot back for the payment of agricultural subsidies, but if we could decide on these billions ourselves, it is not certain that we would use them for agricultural subsidies, at least not for conventional poison farming. Or another example: we send money to Brussels, which then decides to support a ski slope on Bornholm. Not an evil word about the ski slope on Bornholm, it is undoubtedly a fantastic project, which I believe has the recommendation of none other than the multiple world champion and slalom specialist, Alberto Tomba, who said that he particularly wanted to ski on Bornholm. But if the money had been in our own pockets, we could have let Bornholm decide for themselves whether their most burning desire in the world now was to have a ski slope. That is why we would very much like to discuss how to change that system. And that is why it is quite logical, and it is probably also very reasonable, that the richest countries pay more than the poorest countries, if you want to be a member of the club. And if you look at the net contribution from 2015, it is not the case that Denmark is making the most of our GNP. There we are being surpassed by countries such as Belgium, the Netherlands, Germany, the United Kingdom and Sweden. On the other hand, we are fully involved in discussing whether it is reasonable for countries to make very large contributions from the European Union but, at the same time, to reject any kind of solidarity effort, for example. when it was about solving the task of providing shelter for the many refugees who had come to Europe, among other things. fleeing the civil war in Syria. From the Enhedslisten, we can surely find something more sensible to spend than to stop 50 billion. kr. a year in the authoritarian regime in Hungary and over 100 billion. kr. in Poland. But that is, of course, another discussion. In conclusion, I would like to sum up that we fully share the proposers' wish for less money for the EU, but in our view the solution is not a special Danish rebate, but a fundamental change in the EU economy.

ParlaMint-DK_20170504195237	When Mrs Kirsten Normann, in her explanatory statement for the question, mentions the National Hospital's maternity department, I look forward to Mrs Kirsten Normann Andersen in her rapporteur's speech making a careful examination of the resources available for the National Hospital's maternity department, in relation to how many births there have been and what historical developments there have been over time.
