After all, negotiations between the government and an external party are taking place exactly between the government and the external party, and we therefore have a situation in which I shall discuss with Danske Regions and their negotiating staff what the economy should be like. That is why it is limited what I can tell you today about a future agreement, because an agreement means that two parties have agreed. However, in line with what has just been said, I would like to outline the overall framework for the forthcoming economic negotiations. The starting point for the economic negotiations on the overall framework for the public economy and the region's economy for 2018 shall be determined taking into account economic development. If we look at the Danish economy as a whole, we can see that right now we are experiencing a recovery with increasing growth, falling unemployment and strong growth in employment. In each of the years after 2009, we have had growth in our GDP. Private employment is rising sharply and unemployment is at its lowest level for 40 years, except in 2007 and 2008, when the Danish economy was overheated. This is basically a positive development and something that we should welcome, not least as it also means that more and more people are getting into jobs and thus moving from public support to self-sufficiency. It is still important to stress that we continue to have deficits in public finances and that, although the prosperity of the Danish economy in isolation strengthens the public balance, it is a bottom-up task for us to gradually reduce the deficit in the coming years. This is not least necessary in the light of the demographic developments that we can see, with more elderly people and fewer people standing for support in the coming years. It is important to stress here that the government wants to pursue a responsible economic policy. This also means that fiscal policy must be organized accordingly. There is currently a need for restraint in fiscal policy so that economic policy fits the business cycle. Indeed, we must ensure that we have a sustainable recovery in the Danish economy and we must address the risks of overheating and bottlenecks in the labour market. As I have said before, we should not go back to the '00s when the crisis got worse because we saw a period when public spending ran out of control. At the same time, the Government's ambition is that total public expenditure should be a smaller part of the economy. The Government's objective is that basic growth in public consumption should, as a starting point, be 0.3 per cent. In addition, there are the priorities that will be possible through reform, for example. an SU reform. Reforms must help to transform the public sector so that it contributes where it is most needed. Overall, the message is that the financial framework for the public sector in the coming years will be tight, even if we succeed in further increasing the priority space through reforms. Sustainability is a necessary condition, as a healthy public economy is a prerequisite for us to create growth and development throughout Denmark. It is also necessary for us to have the possibility of maintaining a strong welfare society in the future. Of course, in the negotiations on the economy of the regions, it is also important to look at the overall economic situation in the health sector and for the regions. There is no doubt that health is a priority area for the government. In 2016, the V government added 2.4 billion to health. DKr, of which the regions received the vast majority, including for information purposes at increasing cost of medicine. In addition, the 2017 Finance Act included a Cancer Plan IV, which totals nearly EUR 15 billion. kr. for 2017-2020, including: with the aim of increasing the capacity for diagnostics and treatment in the regions. Finally, if we look down in the accounts of the regions for 2016, we can see that it also looks quite reasonable. The region's accounts for 2016 are therefore approx. 300 million kr. in the 2016 budget of approx. 107,7 billion kr. In fact, it is a lower consumption that is greater than the lower consumption that was in 2015. At the same time, there are no longer indications of a marked pressure on medical costs and activity increases in hospitals, as has been the case in previous years. It dampens the pressure for additional funds for the hospitals. This must be compared with the opportunities available for better capacity utilisation and better workflows. Among other things: the new super hospitals will be completed in the years to come, and this means great additional benefits, because the new constructions make it possible to organise patients' treatment much better for the benefit of patients, but also for the benefit of the economy. The overall picture is therefore a calm and subdued development of expenditure. Another important part of the economy of the regions concerns investment. This includes, among other things: the quality fund-supported hospital buildings, the so-called super-sickhouses, where, towards 2022 overall, priority is given to investments for more than 48 billion. kr. Buildings are of high quality, as they are the cornerstone of the future organisation of our health care, and in recent years, therefore, the region's construction framework has also been historically high. However, construction makes demands, demands great responsibility on the part of the regions, so that we have realistic drainage profiles. This is because, as has already been said, there is a need for adjustments which are in line with a sound stabilisation policy and adapted to the economic situation. In addition, there is currently a global challenge to reduce the public investment already planned in buildings and installations to the levels financed in the projection, or to put it another way: when the government took over the government in 2015, construction projects were planned for 5 billion. kr. more than was financed, and this will have to be adjusted down unless new funding can be obtained by extending the scope through reforms. The message is therefore that the regions must help to reduce public investment to a sustainable level and give priority to quality fund-supported construction at a realistic level. The economic priorities of health care go hand in hand with a requirement for regions to modernise and realise opportunities for efficiency. The health sector is characterised by considerable technological development, new methods of treatment, new hospitals and new medicines. It provides a basis for solving the tasks better and cheaper. In the field of health, the tool is a requirement for overall productivity improvements of 2%. It is supported by a number of analyses of the basis for efficiency improvements and reprioritisation. Then an analysis has been made of the administration of the regions which indicates that the proportion of administrative staff is increasing and represents an increasing proportion of total staff. Therefore, better capacity utilisation, better procurement and a re-prioritisation of administration to patients' health are essential themes for economic negotiations. There is currently a lot of debate on health governance, including a number of stakeholders who have been critical of the productivity requirement. Just to make it absolutely clear: the health area is a central focus area for the government, and we take no money from the hospitals on the contrary. On the contrary, the current new money and more money are being added, so it is a big misunderstanding if anyone believes that the requirement of productivity and activity management are about taking money from someone. The requirement for productivity and activity management shall support the gains which, inter alia, include: is from new technologies and new, better forms of treatment are also being implemented. Having said that, we must, of course, develop and improve governance. Can we, for example, do this? In order to take better account of the overall course of a patient rather than of the individual's hospital visits, can we oblige both hospital and general practice to deliver what is, overall, most beneficial to patients? This is something we are investigating, and that is why the government is now launching a comprehensive review of health governance. We are doing this to ensure that the framework for health development is in place. In the work we will shed light on what is working in the current governance and what can be improved. It will be a thorough work and we will not be jumping to conclusions or falling into easy headlines, which may tend in the debate on health governance. So it is not a small task that is being tackled, and therefore it is important for me to stress that the aim of the government is more quality rather than timing. This means that in our cooperation with Danske Regionser, we will be actively in favour of seeking a model that meets both Danske Regioner's position on finding other and better management tools, but which also guarantees that you really have a hand in business and management. And that requires a piece of work that I think is important to follow through, even more important than doing so within a certain date. In conclusion, I would like to emphasise that we are looking forward to the forthcoming negotiations with the Danish Regions. As I said, the health sector is an important priority for the government, as we have shown in several rounds with the initiatives we have taken and the means we have provided. At the same time, it is also important in the field of health that we make the best use of resources and capacity. That's why we're gonna be together with, uh... Danish Regions ensure that we get as much health as possible for the money. This is central to the continued development of our healthcare services for the benefit of patients.

And we think it's pretty reasonable that there's this pressure. So for us, it is important that we maintain this pressure, that of course we make some demands to increase efficiency. A few years ago I had the pleasure of sitting on the Health Committee for a period of time, and there we visited a hospital in Skåne, which had a Danish hospital director, where productivity had increased quite, quite significantly, far more than 2%. a year. We also talked to the staff of the hospital concerned, and what they said was that improved workflows, which had led to higher productivity, had increased job satisfaction because more time was now spent on what was actually there to treat patients. Less time was spent waiting, fewer frustrations, improved job satisfaction. So to this premise that it automatically leads to poorer working conditions, that there is a productivity requirement, I would say: I am not sure it is that simple. That is not to say that there can be nothing about the requirement of productivity that is inappropriate in relation to the way it is measured, and I hope that the government will also have a dialogue with the regions about this in future negotiations. I can certainly see that there is still room for better use of health resources. If you look at the administrative costs, especially in the Capital Region, you will see that they have increased very, very strongly. And the most important thing must be to ensure that the many, many billions, over 100 billion. kr., which is used every year in health care, is used appropriately, is used better. It must be more important to ensure first, that is, before we add more resources. For there are also other important challenges to be solved in Danish society. Thank you.

If you go in and look at patient satisfaction, you can see that it is high in relation to hospitalization in Danish hospitals. If you look at the waiting times in psychiatry, you can see that since 2010, when an additional effort was made, they have been falling and they continue to be falling. In 2016, the waiting time for planned operations was declared to be record low, so if you go out and look at the quality of it, if you measure the quality of survival of patients, you can see that the survival rate for cancer is increasing and has been increasing since 2001 onwards and also increasing since 2007. If you look at the mortality rate, you can see that it has decreased in all regions, and the differences in mortality rates between regions have also decreased. There are shorter and better courses in cancer treatment. It is the case that if you look at the heart treatment, you can see that there is better heart treatment and it saves lives. I haven't been doing statistics in the hours since we started this discussion. I didn't ask the government, the Ministry of Finance, to do some kind of beauty painting either. I have just taken the hospital owners' own inventory after 9 years with a 2-percent efficiency requirement and tried to look at what the health system, the hospital owners say, has meant for quality It has been that things are going better. It is better for patients, better for mortality, better for the economy. Does that mean everything is perfect? No, it doesn't. This is also why the government, together with the Social Democratic Party, has put forward a proposal for adoption which calls for a review of the governance in the field of health to clarify what works and does not work in the current governance and, in this context, to propose better governance in the future. Have we made a huge concession today? Are there breaking news, yellow beams and new front pages in tomorrow's newspapers? I hope not, because if so, you will have slept per hour since my predecessor last summer made an economic agreement with Danske Regions, where you set it in motion here: a series of attempts to find out exactly what we can do to improve quality, what we can do to improve the way we do financial management. I have repeatedly asked in hospitals, in regional councils, in the Ministry of Finance, in the Ministry of Health: what is the result? What can I take out of the studies, the experiments that have been started? The answers so far have been: nothing yet, because they have not yet been completed, because they have not been completed nor have they been completed, nor have they been carried out by the Danish Regions or specific hospitals themselves. They do not come and tell me that they have been completed so that they can be concluded on the basis of them. Therefore, I say that I do not think it is right to jump from a steering tool that we have, as we know, and which has helped deliver quite excellent results according to Danske Regions themselves. It is not right to just say that you have to scrap it without knowing what to replace. I fully recognise that there are problematic elements in the current governance model. What we have heard in the past is that the management model can produce a certain type of treatment, unlike something that is actually correct in patient terms, or that more contacts can be maintained with the individual patient than there is evidence of health care, are examples of something that can be better done. We fully acknowledge that. That is why, together with the Social Democracy, the Left, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservative People's Party, we have also written that it must be examined how to achieve better governance, what works and what does not work. Because I fear some people are throwing the baby out with the bath water. Because you can see that there are individual problems, you are putting everything aside. I would just like to conclude by saying that, in a rather crooked way, I would have said that I take note of the difference between the proposals for adoption that have been made. They're pretty much the same. Well, I can't be remembered and I've only been in here for 19 years that there's been such a similar proposal for adoption in a question like these. However, I noted that the proposal from the Alternative and Enhedslisten neither respects the contractual system nor takes an interest in keeping within the framework of a responsible economic policy. I'm not surprised. Thank you.
